Me:

1) I need you to create three things for me.

2) Firstly, I need a CloudFormation template that will create a new VPC with three public and three private subnets and an EC2 machine having Amazon Linux 2023 with Amazon Kiro Web Version installed in it.

3) Also install an MSK Cluster with the option to install either Provisioned or Serverless, based on an input parameter

4) If Provisioned is selected by the user, install the MSK Cluster inside the VPC with 3 brokers in the three private subnets.

5) On the EC2 machine install the following - latest Amazon Corretto Java version supported by AWS Lambda, nmap-ncat, git client, aws cli latest version, jq, docker (install and start the docker daemon), Apache Maven latest, AWS SAM, Kafka client latest version.

6) MSK Clients will need to be configured to use IAM authentication, so install the necessary jar file in the Kafka client and also create a client.properties file with the correct parameters that can be used by Kafka clients to connect to the MSK cluster

7) Make sure a security group is created for clients running on the EC2 machine to be able to connect to the MSK cluster

8) Make sure the EC2 machine instance role has policies that allow clients running on the EC2 machine to connect to the MSK cluster

9) The EC2 machine's security group should also allow connecting to the machine using EC2 Instance Connect

10) Now please create the CloudFormation template with the above instructions. I will then provide you instructions for the other two things that I need you to do for me

   KafkaTopicName:
     Description: Name of the Kafka topic to create
     Type: String
     Default: my-kafka-topic

the stack.

2. Enhanced the EC2 UserData script to:
   • Export the Kafka topic name to the environment variables file
   • Create a more robust script (create-kafka-topic.sh) that:
     • Checks if the topic already exists before creating it
     • Creates the topic with 3 partitions as requested
     • Provides error handling and verification
     • Outputs detailed information about the created topic
   • Execute the topic creation script automatically during instance 
initialization
   • Save the output to a log file for troubleshooting

3. Added the Kafka topic name to the CloudFormation outputs for easy reference 
after stack creation.

The script now automatically creates the Kafka topic with the name provided by 
the user parameter during the CloudFormation stack deployment. The topic will 
have 3 partitions as requested, and the replication factor is set to 3 (which is
appropriate for a 3-broker MSK cluster).

The topic creation process is logged to /home/ec2-user/topic-creation-result.log
on the EC2 instance, so users can check the results of the topic creation 
process.

+     9:  * Utility class to generate fake data using JavaFaker library.
+    10:  */
+    11: public class DataGenerator {
+    12:     private final Faker faker;
+    13: 
+    14:     public DataGenerator() {
+    15:         this.faker = new Faker(Locale.US);
+    16:     }
+    17: 
+    18:     /**
+    19:      * Generates a Person object with random fake data.
+    20:      *
+    21:      * @return Person object with fake data
+    22:      */
+    23:     public Person generatePerson() {
+    24:         // Generate personal information
+    25:         String firstName = faker.name().firstName();
+    26:         String lastName = faker.name().lastName();
+    27:         String streetAddress = faker.address().streetAddress();
+    28:         String city = faker.address().city();
+    29:         String state = faker.address().state();
+    30:         String county = faker.address().county();
+    31:         String zipCode = faker.address().zipCode();
+    32:         String phoneNumber = faker.phoneNumber().phoneNumber();
+    33:         String emailAddress = firstName.toLowerCase() + "." + lastName.toLowerCase() + "@" + faker.internet().domainName();
+    34: 
+    35:         // Generate employer information
+    36:         String employerName = faker.company().name();
+    37:         String employerAddress = faker.address().fullAddress();
+    38:         String employerPhoneNumber = faker.phoneNumber().phoneNumber();
+    39:         String employerWebsite = "www." + employerName.toLowerCase().replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z0-9]", "") + "." + faker.internet().domainSuffix();
+    40: 
+    41:         return new Person(
+    42:                 firstName,
+    43:                 lastName,
+    44:                 streetAddress,
+    45:                 city,
+    46:                 state,
+    47:                 county,
+    48:                 zipCode,
+    49:                 phoneNumber,
+    50:                 emailAddress,
+    51:                 employerName,
+    52:                 employerAddress,
+    53:                 employerPhoneNumber,
+    54:                 employerWebsite
+    55:         );
+    56:     }
+    57: }

> Now, let's create the main Kafka producer class:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/kafka-producer/src/main/java/com/example/kafka/KafkaProducer.java

+      1: package com.example.kafka;
+      2: 
+      3: import com.example.kafka.model.Person;
+      4: import com.example.kafka.util.DataGenerator;
+      5: import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.ObjectMapper;
+      6: import org.apache.commons.cli.*;
+      7: import org.apache.kafka.clients.producer.*;
+      8: import org.slf4j.Logger;
+      9: import org.slf4j.LoggerFactory;
+     10: 
+     11: import java.io.FileInputStream;
+     12: import java.io.IOException;
+     13: import java.util.Properties;
+     14: import java.util.UUID;
+     15: import java.util.concurrent.CountDownLatch;
+     16: import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;
+     17: 
+     18: /**
+     19:  * Kafka Producer application that sends fake person data to a Kafka topic.
+     20:  */
+     21: public class KafkaProducer {
+     22:     private static final Logger logger = LoggerFactory.getLogger(KafkaProducer.class);
+     23:     private static final ObjectMapper objectMapper = new ObjectMapper();
+     24:     private static final DataGenerator dataGenerator = new DataGenerator();
+     25: 
+     26:     public static void main(String[] args) {
+     27:         // Parse command line arguments
+     28:         CommandLine cmd = parseCommandLineArgs(args);
+     29:         if (cmd == null) {
+     30:             return;
+     31:         }
+     32: 
+     33:         String propertiesFile = cmd.getOptionValue("properties");
+     34:         String topic = cmd.getOptionValue("topic");
+     35:         String batchId = cmd.getOptionValue("batch-id", UUID.randomUUID().toString());
+     36:         int messageCount = Integer.parseInt(cmd.getOptionValue("count", "10"));
+     37: 
+     38:         logger.info("Starting Kafka Producer with the following parameters:");
+     39:         logger.info("Properties File: {}", propertiesFile);
+     40:         logger.info("Topic: {}", topic);
+     41:         logger.info("Batch ID: {}", batchId);
+     42:         logger.info("Message Count: {}", messageCount);
+     43: 
+     44:         // Load Kafka properties
+     45:         Properties kafkaProps = loadProperties(propertiesFile);
+     46:         if (kafkaProps == null) {
+     47:             return;
+     48:         }
+     49: 
+     50:         // Create Kafka producer
+     51:         try (Producer<String, String> producer = new org.apache.kafka.clients.producer.KafkaProducer<>(kafkaProps)) {
+     52:             sendMessages(producer, topic, batchId, messageCount);
+     53:         } catch (Exception e) {
+     54:             logger.error("Error creating or using Kafka producer", e);
+     55:         }
+     56:     }
+     57: 
+     58:     /**
+     59:      * Parse command line arguments.
+     60:      *
+     61:      * @param args Command line arguments
+     62:      * @return CommandLine object or null if parsing fails
+     63:      */
+     64:     private static CommandLine parseCommandLineArgs(String[] args) {
+     65:         Options options = new Options();
+     66: 
+     67:         Option propertiesOption = Option.builder("p")
+     68:                 .longOpt("properties")
+     69:                 .desc("Properties file with Kafka configuration")
+     70:                 .hasArg()
+     71:                 .required(true)
+     72:                 .build();
+     73: 
+     74:         Option topicOption = Option.builder("t")
+     75:                 .longOpt("topic")
+     76:                 .desc("Kafka topic to send messages to")
+     77:                 .hasArg()
+     78:                 .required(true)
+     79:                 .build();
+     80: 
+     81:         Option batchIdOption = Option.builder("b")
+     82:                 .longOpt("batch-id")
+     83:                 .desc("Unique identifier for this batch of messages")
+     84:                 .hasArg()
+     85:                 .required(false)
+     86:                 .build();
+     87: 
+     88:         Option countOption = Option.builder("c")
+     89:                 .longOpt("count")
+     90:                 .desc("Number of messages to send")
+     91:                 .hasArg()
+     92:                 .required(false)
+     93:                 .build();
+     94: 
+     95:         options.addOption(propertiesOption);
+     96:         options.addOption(topicOption);
+     97:         options.addOption(batchIdOption);
+     98:         options.addOption(countOption);
+     99: 
+    100:         CommandLineParser parser = new DefaultParser();
+    101:         HelpFormatter formatter = new HelpFormatter();
+    102: 
+    103:         try {
+    104:             return parser.parse(options, args);
+    105:         } catch (ParseException e) {
+    106:             logger.error("Error parsing command line arguments: {}", e.getMessage());
+    107:             formatter.printHelp("KafkaProducer", options);
+    108:             return null;
+    109:         }
+    110:     }
+    111: 
+    112:     /**
+    113:      * Load Kafka properties from a file.
+    114:      *
+    115:      * @param propertiesFile Path to properties file
+    116:      * @return Properties object or null if loading fails
+    117:      */
+    118:     private static Properties loadProperties(String propertiesFile) {
+    119:         Properties props = new Properties();
+    120:         try (FileInputStream fis = new FileInputStream(propertiesFile)) {
+    121:             props.load(fis);
+    122:             logger.info("Loaded Kafka properties from {}", propertiesFile);
+    123:             return props;
+    124:         } catch (IOException e) {
+    125:             logger.error("Error loading properties file: {}", e.getMessage());
+    126:             return null;
+    127:         }
+    128:     }
+    129: 
+    130:     /**
+    131:      * Send messages to Kafka topic.
+    132:      *
+    133:      * @param producer     Kafka producer
+    134:      * @param topic        Topic to send messages to
+    135:      * @param batchId      Unique identifier for this batch
+    136:      * @param messageCount Number of messages to send
+    137:      */
+    138:     private static void sendMessages(Producer<String, String> producer, String topic, String batchId, int messageCount) {
+    139:         logger.info("Sending {} messages to topic {} with batch ID {}", messageCount, topic, batchId);
+    140: 
+    141:         final CountDownLatch latch = new CountDownLatch(messageCount);
+    142:         final AtomicInteger successCount = new AtomicInteger(0);
+    143:         final AtomicInteger failureCount = new AtomicInteger(0);
+    144: 
+    145:         for (int i = 0; i < messageCount; i++) {
+    146:             try {
+    147:                 // Generate fake person data
+    148:                 Person person = dataGenerator.generatePerson();
+    149:                 
+    150:                 // Convert to JSON
+    151:                 String jsonData = objectMapper.writeValueAsString(person);
+    152:                 
+    153:                 // Create a unique key for each message
+    154:                 String key = batchId + "-" + i;
+    155:                 
+    156:                 // Create ProducerRecord
+    157:                 ProducerRecord<String, String> record = new ProducerRecord<>(topic, key, jsonData);
+    158:                 
+    159:                 // Send message asynchronously
+    160:                 producer.send(record, (metadata, exception) -> {
+    161:                     if (exception == null) {
+    162:                         successCount.incrementAndGet();
+    163:                         logger.debug("Message sent successfully: topic={}, partition={}, offset={}",
+    164:                                 metadata.topic(), metadata.partition(), metadata.offset());
+    165:                     } else {
+    166:                         failureCount.incrementAndGet();
+    167:                         logger.error("Error sending message: {}", exception.getMessage());
+    168:                     }
+    169:                     latch.countDown();
+    170:                 });
+    171:                 
+    172:                 // Log progress every 100 messages
+    173:                 if ((i + 1) % 100 == 0 || i == messageCount - 1) {
+    174:                     logger.info("Progress: sent {}/{} messages", i + 1, messageCount);
+    175:                 }
+    176:             } catch (Exception e) {
+    177:                 logger.error("Error preparing message {}: {}", i, e.getMessage());
+    178:                 latch.countDown();
+    179:                 failureCount.incrementAndGet();
+    180:             }
+    181:         }
+    182: 
+    183:         try {
+    184:             // Wait for all messages to be acknowledged
+    185:             latch.await();
+    186:             logger.info("Finished sending messages. Success: {}, Failures: {}", 
+    187:                     successCount.get(), failureCount.get());
+    188:         } catch (InterruptedException e) {
+    189:             logger.error("Interrupted while waiting for message acknowledgments", e);
+    190:             Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
+    191:         }
+    192:     }
+    193: }

🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/kafka-producer/README.md

+     1: # Kafka Producer for MSK
+     2: 
+     3: This Java application generates fake person data and sends it as JSON messages to an Amazon MSK Kafka cluster.
+     4: 
+     5: ## Prerequisites
+     6: 
+     7: - Java 11 or higher
+     8: - Apache Maven
+     9: - Access to an Amazon MSK cluster with IAM authentication enabled
+    10: 
+    11: ## Building the Application
+    12: 
+    13: To build the application, run:
+    14: 
+    15: ```bash
+    16: mvn clean package
+    17: ```
+    18: 
+    19: This will create a JAR file with all dependencies in the `target` directory.
+    20: 
+    21: ## Configuration
+    22: 
+    23: Before running the application, you need to create a properties file with Kafka configuration. A sample file `kafka-client.properties` is provided.
+    24: 
+    25: Update the `bootstrap.servers` property with your MSK bootstrap servers. You can get this from the AWS Console or using the AWS CLI:
+    26: 
+    27: ```bash
+    28: aws kafka get-bootstrap-brokers --cluster-arn YOUR_CLUSTER_ARN --region YOUR_REGION
+    29: ```
+    30: 
+    31: For MSK with IAM authentication, use the `BootstrapBrokerStringSaslIam` value for provisioned clusters or `BootstrapBrokerStringVpcConnectivitySaslIam` for serverless clusters.
+    32: 
+    33: ## Running the Application
+    34: 
+    35: Run the application using the following command:
+    36: 
+    37: ```bash
+    38: java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
+    39:   --properties kafka-client.properties \
+    40:   --topic YOUR_TOPIC_NAME \
+    41:   --batch-id unique-batch-identifier \
+    42:   --count 1000
+    43: ```
+    44: 
+    45: ### Command Line Arguments
+    46: 
+    47: - `--properties` or `-p`: Path to the Kafka properties file (required)
+    48: - `--topic` or `-t`: Kafka topic to send messages to (required)
+    49: - `--batch-id` or `-b`: Unique identifier for this batch of messages (optional, defaults to a random UUID)
+    50: - `--count` or `-c`: Number of messages to send (optional, defaults to 10)
+    51: 
+    52: ## Message Format
+    53: 
+    54: Each message is a JSON object with the following fields:
+    55: 
+    56: - Firstname
+    57: - Lastname
+    58: - StreetAddress
+    59: - City
+    60: - State
+    61: - County
+    62: - ZipCode
+    63: - PhoneNumber
+    64: - EmailAddress
+    65: - EmployerName
+    66: - EmployerAddress
+    67: - EmployerPhoneNumber
+    68: - EmployerWebsite
+    69: 
+    70: ## Example
+    71: 
+    72: ```bash
+    73: # Send 100 messages to the 'customer-data' topic
+    74: java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
+    75:   --properties kafka-client.properties \
+    76:   --topic customer-data \
+    77:   --batch-id batch-2023-07-19 \
+    78:   --count 100
+    79: ```
+    80: 
+    81: ## Troubleshooting
+    82: 
+    83: If you encounter issues with IAM authentication, ensure:
+    84: 
+    85: 1. The EC2 instance has the correct IAM role with MSK permissions
+    86: 2. The MSK cluster has IAM authentication enabled
+    87: 3. The security groups allow traffic between the EC2 instance and MSK cluster
+    88: 4. The AWS MSK IAM Auth JAR is in the classpath (included in the packaged JAR)

+     1: #!/bin/bash
+     2: 
+     3: # Build the application
+     4: echo "Building the Kafka Producer application..."
+     5: mvn clean package
+     6: 
+     7: if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
+     8:     echo "Build failed. Please check the errors above."
+     9:     exit 1
+    10: fi
+    11: 
+    12: echo "Build successful!"
+    13: 
+    14: # Check if we have the required arguments
+    15: if [ "$#" -lt 2 ]; then
+    16:     echo "Usage: $0 <topic-name> <message-count> [batch-id]"
+    17:     echo "Example: $0 my-topic 100 batch-001"
+    18:     exit 1
+    19: fi
+    20: 
+    21: TOPIC=$1
+    22: COUNT=$2
+    23: BATCH_ID=${3:-$(uuidgen)}
+    24: 
+    25: # Check if properties file exists
+    26: PROPERTIES_FILE="kafka-client.properties"
+    27: if [ ! -f "$PROPERTIES_FILE" ]; then
+    28:     echo "Error: Properties file $PROPERTIES_FILE not found."
+    29:     echo "Please create this file with your Kafka configuration."
+    30:     exit 1
+    31: fi
+    32: 
+    33: # Run the application
+    34: echo "Running Kafka Producer with:"
+    35: echo "  Topic: $TOPIC"
+    36: echo "  Count: $COUNT"
+    37: echo "  Batch ID: $BATCH_ID"
+    38: echo ""
+    39: 
+    40: java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
+    41:   --properties "$PROPERTIES_FILE" \
+    42:   --topic "$TOPIC" \
+    43:   --batch-id "$BATCH_ID" \
+    44:   --count "$COUNT"

## Kafka Producer Project Structure

1. Maven Project Setup:
   • Created a Maven project with all necessary dependencies:
     • Kafka clients
     • JavaFaker for generating realistic fake data
     • Jackson for JSON processing
     • AWS MSK IAM authentication library
     • Apache Commons CLI for command-line parsing

2. Key Components:

   • **Person.java**: A model class representing a person with all the required 
fields:
     • Firstname, Lastname, StreetAddress, City, State, County, ZipCode, 
PhoneNumber, EmailAddress
     • EmployerName, EmployerAddress, EmployerPhoneNumber, EmployerWebsite

   • **DataGenerator.java**: Utility class that uses JavaFaker to generate 
realistic looking fake data for all fields.

   • **KafkaProducer.java**: Main class that:
     • Parses command-line arguments
     • Loads Kafka properties from a file
     • Generates fake person data
     • Converts data to JSON
     • Sends messages to the specified Kafka topic
     • Tracks success/failure of message delivery

3. Configuration and Documentation:
   • Sample kafka-client.properties file with MSK IAM authentication settings
   • Comprehensive README.md with usage instructions
   • Build and run script to simplify execution

## How to Use the Producer

1. Build the application:
  
bash
   cd /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/kafka-producer
   mvn clean package
   


2. Update the properties file with your MSK bootstrap servers:
  
   bootstrap.servers=YOUR_MSK_BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS
   

3. Run the application using the provided script:
  bash
   ./build-and-run.sh your-topic-name 1000 your-batch-id
   
  
  Or manually:
  
bash
   java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
     --properties kafka-client.properties \
     --topic your-topic-name \
     --batch-id your-batch-id \
     --count 1000
   


The application will generate the specified number of messages with realistic 
fake data and send them to your MSK cluster. Each message will be a JSON string 
containing all the required fields.

1) Please create an AWS SAM project that will deploy a lambda function written in Java and use Maven

 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-dynamodb-processor/template.yaml

+      1: AWSTemplateFormatVersion: '2010-09-09'
+      2: Transform: AWS::Serverless-2016-10-31
+      3: Description: >
+      4:   MSK to DynamoDB Processor
+      5:   
+      6:   SAM Template for processing Kafka messages from MSK and storing them in DynamoDB
+      7: 
+      8: Parameters:
+      9:   MSKClusterArn:
+     10:     Type: String
+     11:     Description: ARN of the MSK Cluster to use as event source
+     12:     
+     13:   KafkaTopicName:
+     14:     Type: String
+     15:     Description: Name of the Kafka topic to consume messages from
+     16:     
+     17:   Environment:
+     18:     Type: String
+     19:     Default: dev
+     20:     Description: Environment name (dev, test, prod)
+     21:     
+     22:   DynamoDBTableName:
+     23:     Type: String
+     24:     Default: PersonData
+     25:     Description: Name of the DynamoDB table to store person data
+     26: 
+     27: Globals:

+     31:     Tracing: Active
+     32:     Environment:

+     35:         POWERTOOLS_METRICS_NAMESPACE: MSKDynamoDBProcessor
+     36:         LOG_LEVEL: INFO
+     37:         DYNAMODB_TABLE_NAME: !Ref DynamoDBTableName
+     38: 
+     39: Resources:
+     40:   # DynamoDB Table to store person data
+     41:   PersonDataTable:
+     42:     Type: AWS::DynamoDB::Table
+     43:     Properties:
+     44:       TableName: !Ref DynamoDBTableName
+     45:       BillingMode: PAY_PER_REQUEST
+     46:       AttributeDefinitions:
+     47:         - AttributeName: id
+     48:           AttributeType: S
+     49:       KeySchema:

> Now, let's create the DynamoDB service class:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-dynamodb-processor/src/main/java/com/example/processor/service/DynamoDBService.java

+     1: package com.example.processor.service;
+     2: 
+     3: import com.example.processor.model.Person;
+     4: import software.amazon.awssdk.enhanced.dynamodb.DynamoDbEnhancedClient;
+     5: import software.amazon.awssdk.enhanced.dynamodb.DynamoDbTable;
+     6: import software.amazon.awssdk.enhanced.dynamodb.TableSchema;
+     7: import software.amazon.awssdk.regions.Region;
+     8: import software.amazon.awssdk.services.dynamodb.DynamoDbClient;
+     9: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.logging.Logging;
+    10: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.metrics.Metrics;
+    11: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.tracing.Tracing;
+    12: 
+    13: /**
+    14:  * Service class for DynamoDB operations.
+    15:  */
+    16: public class DynamoDBService {
+    17:     private final DynamoDbEnhancedClient enhancedClient;
+    18:     private final DynamoDbTable<Person> personTable;
+    19: 
+    20:     /**
+    21:      * Constructor that initializes the DynamoDB client and table.
+    22:      *
+    23:      * @param tableName The name of the DynamoDB table
+    24:      */
+    25:     public DynamoDBService(String tableName) {
+    26:         // Create DynamoDB client
+    27:         DynamoDbClient ddbClient = DynamoDbClient.builder()
+    28:                 .region(Region.of(System.getenv("AWS_REGION")))
+    29:                 .build();
+    30: 
+    31:         // Create enhanced client
+    32:         this.enhancedClient = DynamoDbEnhancedClient.builder()
+    33:                 .dynamoDbClient(ddbClient)
+    34:                 .build();
+    35: 
+    36:         // Get a reference to the person table
+    37:         this.personTable = enhancedClient.table(tableName, TableSchema.fromBean(Person.class));
+    38:     }
+    39: 
+    40:     /**
+    41:      * Saves a person to DynamoDB.
+    42:      *
+    43:      * @param person The person to save
+    44:      * @return The saved person
+    45:      */
+    46:     @Tracing
+    47:     @Logging(logEvent = true)
+    48:     @Metrics(namespace = "MSKDynamoDBProcessor", service = "DynamoDBService")
+    49:     public Person savePerson(Person person) {
+    50:         personTable.putItem(person);
+    51:         return person;
+    52:     }
+    53: }

 ↳ Purpose: Creating DynamoDB service class

> Now, let's create the main Lambda handler class:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-dynamodb-processor/src/main/java/com/example/processor/MSKProcessor.java

+      1: package com.example.processor;
+      2: 
+      3: import com.amazonaws.services.lambda.runtime.Context;
+      4: import com.amazonaws.services.lambda.runtime.RequestHandler;
+      5: import com.amazonaws.services.lambda.runtime.events.KafkaEvent;
+      6: import com.amazonaws.services.lambda.runtime.events.KafkaEvent.KafkaEventRecord;
+      7: import com.example.processor.model.Person;
+      8: import com.example.processor.service.DynamoDBService;
+      9: import com.fasterxml.jackson.databind.ObjectMapper;
+     10: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.logging.Logging;
+     11: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.metrics.Metrics;
+     12: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.metrics.MetricsUtils;
+     13: import software.amazon.lambda.powertools.tracing.Tracing;
+     14: 
+     15: import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;
+     16: import java.time.Instant;
+     17: import java.time.format.DateTimeFormatter;
+     18: import java.util.Base64;
+     19: import java.util.Map;
+     20: 
+     21: /**
+     22:  * Lambda function handler for processing Kafka messages from MSK and storing them in DynamoDB.
+     23:  */
+     24: public class MSKProcessor implements RequestHandler<KafkaEvent, Void> {
+     25:     private static final ObjectMapper objectMapper = new ObjectMapper();
+     26:     private static final String TABLE_NAME = System.getenv("DYNAMODB_TABLE_NAME");
+     27:     private final DynamoDBService dynamoDBService;
+     28: 
+     29:     public MSKProcessor() {
+     30:         this.dynamoDBService = new DynamoDBService(TABLE_NAME);
+     31:     }
+     32: 
+     33:     /**
+     34:      * Lambda function handler method.
+     35:      *
+     36:      * @param event   The Kafka event from MSK
+     37:      * @param context The Lambda context
+     38:      * @return void
+     39:      */
+     40:     @Override
+     41:     @Tracing
+     42:     @Logging(logEvent = true)
+     43:     @Metrics(namespace = "MSKDynamoDBProcessor", service = "MSKProcessor")
+     44:     public Void handleRequest(KafkaEvent event, Context context) {
+     45:         // Log the event
+     46:         context.getLogger().log("Received Kafka event with " + event.getRecords().size() + " record(s)");
+     47:         
+     48:         // Process each record
+     49:         for (Map.Entry<String, java.util.List<KafkaEventRecord>> entry : event.getRecords().entrySet()) {
+     50:             String topic = entry.getKey();
+     51:             context.getLogger().log("Processing records from topic: " + topic);
+     52:             
+     53:             for (KafkaEventRecord record : entry.getValue()) {
+     54:                 processRecord(record, context);
+     55:             }
+     56:         }
+     57:         
+     58:         // Publish metrics
+     59:         MetricsUtils.publishMetrics();
+     60:         return null;
+     61:     }
+     62: 
+     63:     /**
+     64:      * Process a single Kafka record.
+     65:      *
+     66:      * @param record  The Kafka record
+     67:      * @param context The Lambda context
+     68:      */
+     69:     @Tracing
+     70:     @Logging(logEvent = true)
+     71:     private void processRecord(KafkaEventRecord record, Context context) {
+     72:         try {
+     73:             // Extract key and value
+     74:             String key = record.getKey() != null ? 
+     75:                     new String(Base64.getDecoder().decode(record.getKey()), StandardCharsets.UTF_8) : null;
+     76:             String value = new String(Base64.getDecoder().decode(record.getValue()), StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
+     77:             
+     78:             context.getLogger().log("Processing record with key: " + key);
+     79:             
+     80:             // Parse JSON into Person object
+     81:             Person person = objectMapper.readValue(value, Person.class);
+     82:             
+     83:             // Extract batch ID from key if available
+     84:             if (key != null && key.contains("-")) {
+     85:                 String batchId = key.substring(0, key.lastIndexOf("-"));
+     86:                 person.setBatchId(batchId);
+     87:             }
+     88:             
+     89:             // Add processing timestamp
+     90:             person.setProcessedTimestamp(DateTimeFormatter.ISO_INSTANT.format(Instant.now()));
+     91:             
+     92:             // Save to DynamoDB
+     93:             dynamoDBService.savePerson(person);
+     94:             
+     95:             // Log success
+     96:             context.getLogger().log("Successfully processed and saved record: " + person.getId());
+     97:             
+     98:             // Record metric
+     99:             MetricsUtils.putMetric("RecordsProcessed", 1, "Count");
+    100:             
+    101:         } catch (Exception e) {
+    102:             // Log error
+    103:             context.getLogger().log("Error processing record: " + e.getMessage());
+    104:             
+    105:             // Record error metric
+    106:             MetricsUtils.putMetric("ProcessingErrors", 1, "Count");
+    107:         }
+    108:     }
+    109: }

+     1: # MSK to DynamoDB Processor
+     2: 
+     3: This AWS SAM project deploys a Lambda function that processes Kafka messages from an MSK cluster and stores the data in a DynamoDB table.
+     4: 
+     5: ## Architecture
+     6: 
+     7: The solution consists of:
+     8: 
+     9: 1. A Lambda function triggered by messages from an MSK Kafka cluster
+    10: 2. A DynamoDB table to store the processed data
+    11: 3. IAM roles and policies for the Lambda function
+    12: 
+    13: ## Prerequisites
+    14: 
+    15: - AWS CLI installed and configured
+    16: - AWS SAM CLI installed
+    17: - Java 11 or higher
+    18: - Apache Maven
+    19: - An existing MSK cluster (created by the CloudFormation template)
+    20: 
+    21: ## Building the Application
+    22: 
+    23: To build the application, run:
+    24: 
+    25: ```bash
+    26: sam build
+    27: ```
+    28: 
+    29: ## Deploying the Application
+    30: 
+    31: To deploy the application, run:
+    32: 
+    33: ```bash
+    34: sam deploy --guided
+    35: ```
+    36: 
+    37: You will be prompted for the following parameters:
+    38: 
+    39: - **Stack Name**: Name of the CloudFormation stack
+    40: - **AWS Region**: Region to deploy to
+    41: - **MSKClusterArn**: ARN of the MSK cluster (from the CloudFormation output)
+    42: - **KafkaTopicName**: Name of the Kafka topic to consume messages from
+    43: - **Environment**: Environment name (dev, test, prod)
+    44: - **DynamoDBTableName**: Name of the DynamoDB table to create
+    45: 
+    46: ## Testing the Application
+    47: 
+    48: 1. Use the Kafka producer application to send messages to the MSK cluster
+    49: 2. The Lambda function will automatically process these messages and store them in DynamoDB
+    50: 3. Check the CloudWatch logs to verify processing
+    51: 4. Query the DynamoDB table to see the stored data
+    52: 
+    53: ## Monitoring
+    54: 
+    55: The Lambda function uses AWS Lambda Powertools for:
+    56: 
+    57: - Structured logging
+    58: - Metrics
+    59: - Tracing
+    60: 
+    61: You can view logs, metrics, and traces in:
+    62: 
+    63: - CloudWatch Logs
+    64: - CloudWatch Metrics
+    65: - AWS X-Ray
+    66: 
+    67: ## Cleanup
+    68: 
+    69: To delete the deployed resources, run:
+    70: 
+    71: ```bash
+    72: sam delete
+    73: ```

> Let's create a deployment script to make it easier to deploy the SAM 
application:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-dynamodb-processor/deploy.sh

+     1: #!/bin/bash
+     2: 
+     3: # Check if MSK Cluster ARN is provided
+     4: if [ -z "$1" ]; then
+     5:     echo "Usage: $0 <msk-cluster-arn> <kafka-topic-name> [dynamodb-table-name]"
+     6:     echo "Example: $0 arn:aws:kafka:us-east-1:123456789012:cluster/my-cluster/abcdef my-topic PersonData"
+     7:     exit 1
+     8: fi
+     9: 
+    10: MSK_CLUSTER_ARN=$1
+    11: KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME=$2
+    12: DYNAMODB_TABLE_NAME=${3:-"PersonData"}
+    13: 
+    14: # Build the SAM application
+    15: echo "Building the SAM application..."
+    16: sam build
+    17: 
+    18: if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
+    19:     echo "Build failed. Please check the errors above."
+    20:     exit 1
+    21: fi
+    22: 
+    23: echo "Build successful!"
+    24: 
+    25: # Deploy the SAM application
+    26: echo "Deploying the SAM application..."
+    27: sam deploy --guided \
+    28:     --parameter-overrides \
+    29:     MSKClusterArn=$MSK_CLUSTER_ARN \
+    30:     KafkaTopicName=$KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME \
+    31:     DynamoDBTableName=$DYNAMODB_TABLE_NAME
+    32: 
+    33: if [ $? -ne 0 ]; then
+    34:     echo "Deployment failed. Please check the errors above."
+    35:     exit 1
+    36: fi
+    37: 
+    38: echo "Deployment successful!"

> ¡Genial! I've created a complete AWS SAM project that processes Kafka messages
from MSK and stores them in DynamoDB. Here's a summary of what I've built:

## MSK to DynamoDB Processor - SAM Project

### Project Structure

1. SAM Template (template.yaml):
   • Defines a Lambda function with MSK as an event source
   • Creates a DynamoDB table to store person data
   • Sets up IAM roles and policies for the Lambda function
   • Configures CloudWatch logging and X-Ray tracing

2. Lambda Function (MSKProcessor.java):
   • Processes Kafka messages from MSK
   • Parses JSON data into Person objects
   • Stores the data in DynamoDB
   • Uses AWS Lambda Powertools for logging, metrics, and tracing

3. DynamoDB Integration:
   • Uses DynamoDB Enhanced Client for simplified object mapping
   • Automatically creates a unique ID for each record
   • Stores all fields from the Kafka message
   • Adds metadata like batch ID and processing timestamp

4. Supporting Components:
   • Person model class with DynamoDB annotations
   • DynamoDB service class for database operations
   • Maven POM file with all required dependencies
   • Deployment script and README with instructions

### Key Features

1. AWS Lambda Powertools Integration:
   • Structured logging of all incoming messages
   • Custom metrics for monitoring processing
   • Distributed tracing with X-Ray

2. Error Handling:
   • Robust error handling for message processing
   • Error metrics for monitoring failures
   • Detailed logging for troubleshooting

3. Deployment Automation:
   • SAM template for infrastructure as code
   • Deployment script for easy deployment
   • Parameter overrides for customization

### How to Deploy

1. Build the SAM application:
  
bash
   cd /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-dynamodb-processor
   sam build
   


2. Deploy using the script:
  
bash
   ./deploy.sh <msk-cluster-arn> <kafka-topic-name> [dynamodb-table-name]
   


  Replace <msk-cluster-arn> with the ARN of your MSK cluster (from the 
CloudFormation output) and <kafka-topic-name> with the name of your Kafka topic.

3. Test the integration:
   • Use the Kafka producer application to send messages to the MSK cluster
   • The Lambda function will automatically process these messages
   • Check CloudWatch logs for processing details
   • Query the DynamoDB table to see the stored data

This completes the end-to-end solution for processing data from MSK to DynamoDB 
using AWS Lambda. The solution is fully integrated with the CloudFormation 
template and Kafka producer application created earlier.

+    105: 
+    106:         // Then
+    107:         List<ProducerRecord<String, String>> history = mockProducer.history();
+    108:         assertEquals(messageCount, history.size(), "Should have sent " + messageCount + " messages");
+    109:         
+    110:         for (int i = 0; i < messageCount; i++) {
+    111:             ProducerRecord<String, String> record = history.get(i);
+    112:             assertEquals(topic, record.topic(), "Topic should match");
+    113:             assertEquals(batchId + "-" + i, record.key(), "Key should match pattern");
+    114:             assertNotNull(record.value(), "Value should not be null");
+    115:             assertTrue(record.value().contains("Firstname"), "Value should contain Firstname field");
+    116:             assertTrue(record.value().contains("Lastname"), "Value should contain Lastname field");
+    117:         }
+    118:     }
+    119: }

   1,   1: # MSK to DynamoDB Processor
   2,   2: 
   3,   3: This AWS SAM project deploys a Lambda function that processes Kafka messages from an MSK cluster and stores the data in a DynamoDB table.
   4,   4: 
   5,   5: ## Architecture
   6,   6: 
   7,   7: The solution consists of:
   8,   8: 
   9,   9: 1. A Lambda function triggered by messages from an MSK Kafka cluster
  10,  10: 2. A DynamoDB table to store the processed data
  11,  11: 3. IAM roles and policies for the Lambda function
  12,  12: 
  13,  13: ## Prerequisites
  14,  14: 
  15,  15: - AWS CLI installed and configured
  16,  16: - AWS SAM CLI installed
  17,  17: - Java 11 or higher
  18,  18: - Apache Maven
+      19: - Docker (for local testing)
  19,  20: - An existing MSK cluster (created by the CloudFormation template)
  20,  21: 
  21,  22: ## Building the Application
  22,  23: 
  23,  24: To build the application, run:
  24,  25: 
  25,  26: ```bash
  26,  27: sam build
  27,  28: ```
  28,  29: 
+      30: ## Local Testing
+      31: 
+      32: This project includes everything you need to test the Lambda function locally:
+      33: 
+      34: 1. **Start DynamoDB Local**:
+      35:    ```bash
+      36:    ./setup-dynamodb-local.sh
+      37:    ```
+      38:    This script starts DynamoDB Local using Docker Compose and creates the required table.
+      39: 
+      40: 2. **Run the Lambda function locally**:
+      41:    ```bash
+      42:    ./test-local.sh
+      43:    ```
+      44:    This script builds the application and invokes the Lambda function with a sample MSK event.
+      45: 
+      46: 3. **Query the local DynamoDB table**:
+      47:    ```bash
+      48:    aws dynamodb scan --table-name PersonData --endpoint-url http://localhost:8000 --region us-east-1
+      49:    ```
+      50: 
+      51: ## Running Unit Tests
+      52: 
+      53: To run the unit tests:
+      54: 
+      55: ```bash
+      56: mvn test
+      57: ```
+      58: 
  29,  59: ## Deploying the Application
  30,  60: 
  31,  61: To deploy the application, run:
  32,  62: 
  33,  63: ```bash
  34,  64: sam deploy --guided
  35,  65: ```
  36,  66: 
  37,  67: You will be prompted for the following parameters:
  38,  68: 
  39,  69: - **Stack Name**: Name of the CloudFormation stack
  40,  70: - **AWS Region**: Region to deploy to
  41,  71: - **MSKClusterArn**: ARN of the MSK cluster (from the CloudFormation output)
  42,  72: - **KafkaTopicName**: Name of the Kafka topic to consume messages from
  43,  73: - **Environment**: Environment name (dev, test, prod)
  44,  74: - **DynamoDBTableName**: Name of the DynamoDB table to create
  45,  75:

> Perfect! I've added comprehensive unit tests and local testing capabilities 
for both the Kafka producer application and the Lambda function. Here's a 
summary of what I've added:

## 1. Unit Tests for Kafka Producer Application

I've created three test classes for the Kafka producer:

1. DataGeneratorTest: Tests the fake data generation functionality
   • Verifies that generated data is valid and not null
   • Checks that generated persons are unique
   • Validates email format contains first and last name

2. PersonTest: Tests the Person model class
   • Verifies JSON serialization with proper field names
   • Tests JSON deserialization from string to object

3. KafkaProducerTest: Tests the main producer class
   • Tests command-line argument parsing
   • Tests properties file loading
   • Tests message sending functionality using a MockProducer

## 2. Unit Tests for Lambda Function

I've created three test classes for the Lambda function:

1. PersonTest: Tests the Person model class for DynamoDB
   • Verifies JSON deserialization
   • Tests automatic ID generation
   • Tests setters and getters

2. DynamoDBServiceTest: Tests the DynamoDB service
   • Uses Mockito to mock DynamoDB clients
   • Tests the savePerson method

3. MSKProcessorTest: Tests the Lambda handler
   • Tests successful message processing
   • Tests error handling during processing
   • Uses a mock DynamoDB service and mock metrics

## 3. SAM Local Testing Setup

I've added everything needed to test the Lambda function locally:

1. Sample Event File: events/msk-event.json with encoded Kafka messages
   • Contains two sample records with realistic data
   • Properly formatted as an MSK event

2. Environment Variables: env.json for local testing
   • Sets up DynamoDB table name
   • Configures Powertools settings

3. Docker Compose: For running DynamoDB Local
   • Sets up a local DynamoDB instance
   • Configures ports and volumes

4. Setup Script: setup-dynamodb-local.sh
   • Starts DynamoDB Local
   • Creates the required table

5. Test Script: test-local.sh
   • Builds the SAM application
   • Invokes the function with the sample event

## How to Use the Local Testing Setup

1. Start DynamoDB Local:
  bash
   ./setup-dynamodb-local.sh
   

2. Run the Lambda function locally:
  bash
   ./test-local.sh
   

3. Query the local DynamoDB table:
  
bash
   aws dynamodb scan --table-name PersonData --endpoint-url http://localhost:8000 --region us-east-1
   


This setup allows you to develop and test the Lambda function locally before 
deploying it to AWS, which can significantly speed up the development process 
and reduce costs.

The README has also been updated with detailed instructions for local testing 
and running unit tests.

🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/README.md

+      1: # MSK to DynamoDB End-to-End Project
+      2: 
+      3: This project demonstrates an end-to-end serverless data processing pipeline using Amazon MSK (Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka) and AWS Lambda to process data and store it in Amazon DynamoDB.
+      4: 
+      5: ## Architecture
+      6: 
+      7: ![Architecture Diagram](https://via.placeholder.com/800x400?text=MSK+to+DynamoDB+Architecture)
+      8: 
+      9: The solution consists of:
+     10: 
+     11: 1. **Infrastructure Layer**:
+     12:    - Amazon VPC with public and private subnets
+     13:    - Amazon MSK cluster (Provisioned or Serverless)
+     14:    - EC2 instance with development tools
+     15: 
+     16: 2. **Producer Layer**:
+     17:    - Java-based Kafka producer application
+     18:    - Generates fake person data using JavaFaker
+     19:    - Sends JSON messages to MSK
+     20: 
+     21: 3. **Processing Layer**:
+     22:    - AWS Lambda function triggered by MSK events
+     23:    - Processes Kafka messages
+     24:    - Stores data in DynamoDB
+     25: 
+     26: ## Repository Structure
+     27: 
+     28: ```
+     29: /
+     30: ├── msk-environment-template.yaml    # CloudFormation template for infrastructure
+     31: ├── kafka-producer/                  # Kafka producer application
+     32: │   ├── pom.xml                      # Maven project file
+     33: │   ├── src/                         # Source code
+     34: │   └── README.md                    # Producer documentation
+     35: └── msk-dynamodb-processor/          # Lambda function (SAM project)
+     36:     ├── template.yaml                # SAM template
+     37:     ├── src/                         # Source code
+     38:     └── README.md                    # Lambda documentation
+     39: ```
+     40: 
+     41: ## Prerequisites
+     42: 
+     43: - AWS Account
+     44: - AWS CLI installed and configured
+     45: - Git client
+     46: - Java 11 or higher (for local development)
+     47: - Maven (for local development)
+     48: 
+     49: ## Deployment Instructions
+     50: 
+     51: ### 1. Deploy Infrastructure
+     52: 
+     53: 1. Clone this repository:
+     54:    ```bash
+     55:    git clone https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git
+     56:    cd msk-dynamodb-project
+     57:    ```
+     58: 
+     59: 2. Deploy the CloudFormation template:
+     60:    ```bash
+     61:    aws cloudformation create-stack \
+     62:      --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure \
+     63:      --template-body file://msk-environment-template.yaml \
+     64:      --parameters \
+     65:        ParameterKey=EnvironmentName,ParameterValue=MSKEnvironment \
+     66:        ParameterKey=KafkaTopicName,ParameterValue=person-data \
+     67:        ParameterKey=MSKClusterType,ParameterValue=Provisioned \
+     68:        ParameterKey=GitHubRepoURL,ParameterValue=https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git \
+     69:      --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM
+     70:    ```
+     71: 
+     72: 3. Wait for the stack to complete:
+     73:    ```bash
+     74:    aws cloudformation wait stack-create-complete --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure
+     75:    ```
+     76: 
+     77: 4. Get the EC2 instance public IP:
+     78:    ```bash
+     79:    aws cloudformation describe-stacks \
+     80:      --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure \
+     81:      --query "Stacks[0].Outputs[?OutputKey=='EC2PublicIP'].OutputValue" \
+     82:      --output text
+     83:    ```
+     84: 
+     85: ### 2. Connect to EC2 Instance
+     86: 
+     87: 1. Connect to the EC2 instance using EC2 Instance Connect:
+     88:    ```bash
+     89:    aws ec2-instance-connect ssh --instance-id $(aws cloudformation describe-stacks \
+     90:      --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure \
+     91:      --query "Stacks[0].Outputs[?OutputKey=='EC2InstanceId'].OutputValue" \
+     92:      --output text)
+     93:    ```
+     94: 
+     95:    Or use the AWS Console: EC2 > Instances > Select your instance > Connect > EC2 Instance Connect
+     96: 
+     97: 2. Run the setup script:
+     98:    ```bash
+     99:    ./setup-project.sh
+    100:    ```
+    101: 
+    102: ### 3. Deploy the Lambda Function
+    103: 
+    104: 1. On the EC2 instance, navigate to the SAM project:
+    105:    ```bash
+    106:    cd ~/project/msk-dynamodb-processor
+    107:    ```
+    108: 
+    109: 2. Deploy the SAM application:
+    110:    ```bash
+    111:    sam deploy --guided
+    112:    ```
+    113: 
+    114:    The parameters will be pre-filled from the CloudFormation outputs.
+    115: 
+    116: ### 4. Generate and Send Kafka Messages
+    117: 
+    118: 1. On the EC2 instance, navigate to the Kafka producer:
+    119:    ```bash
+    120:    cd ~/project/kafka-producer
+    121:    ```
+    122: 
+    123: 2. Run the producer to send messages:
+    124:    ```bash
+    125:    ./build-and-run.sh person-data 100 batch-001
+    126:    ```
+    127: 
+    128:    This will send 100 messages to the "person-data" topic with batch ID "batch-001".
+    129: 
+    130: ### 5. Verify Data in DynamoDB
+    131: 
+    132: 1. Check that the Lambda function processed the messages:
+    133:    ```bash
+    134:    aws logs describe-log-streams \
+    135:      --log-group-name /aws/lambda/msk-dynamodb-processor-MSKProcessorFunction-XXXX
+    136:    ```
+    137: 
+    138: 2. Query the DynamoDB table:
+    139:    ```bash
+    140:    aws dynamodb scan --table-name PersonData --limit 10
+    141:    ```
+    142: 
+    143: ## Local Development
+    144: 
+    145: ### Kafka Producer
+    146: 
+    147: 1. Clone the repository:
+    148:    ```bash
+    149:    git clone https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git
+    150:    cd msk-dynamodb-project/kafka-producer
+    151:    ```
+    152: 
+    153: 2. Build the application:
+    154:    ```bash
+    155:    mvn clean package
+    156:    ```
+    157: 
+    158: 3. Update the `kafka-client.properties` file with your MSK bootstrap servers.
+    159: 
+    160: 4. Run the producer:
+    161:    ```bash
+    162:    java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
+    163:      --properties kafka-client.properties \
+    164:      --topic your-topic-name \
+    165:      --batch-id your-batch-id \
+    166:      --count 10
+    167:    ```
+    168: 
+    169: ### Lambda Function
+    170: 
+    171: 1. Navigate to the SAM project:
+    172:    ```bash
+    173:    cd msk-dynamodb-project/msk-dynamodb-processor
+    174:    ```
+    175: 
+    176: 2. Run unit tests:
+    177:    ```bash
+    178:    mvn test
+    179:    ```
+    180: 
+    181: 3. Test locally with DynamoDB Local:
+    182:    ```bash
+    183:    ./setup-dynamodb-local.sh
+    184:    ./test-local.sh
+    185:    ```
+    186: 
+    187: ## Cleanup
+    188: 
+    189: 1. Delete the SAM application:
+    190:    ```bash
+    191:    cd ~/project/msk-dynamodb-processor
+    192:    sam delete
+    193:    ```
+    194: 
+    195: 2. Delete the CloudFormation stack:
+    196:    ```bash
+    197:    aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure
+    198:    ```
+    199: 
+    200: ## Security Considerations
+    201: 
+    202: - The EC2 instance has an IAM role with permissions to deploy the SAM application
+    203: - MSK is configured with IAM authentication
+    204: - The Lambda function has least-privilege permissions to access DynamoDB
+    205: - All connections to MSK use TLS encryption
+    206: 
+    207: ## Troubleshooting
+    208: 
+    209: - **Kafka Producer Issues**: Check the Kafka client properties and ensure the bootstrap servers are correct
+    210: - **Lambda Function Issues**: Check CloudWatch Logs for error messages
+    211: - **MSK Connectivity**: Verify security groups allow traffic between EC2 and MSK
+    212: - **DynamoDB Permissions**: Ensure the Lambda execution role has permissions to access DynamoDB
+    213:

+      1: # MSK to DynamoDB End-to-End Project
+      2: 
+      3: This project demonstrates an end-to-end serverless data processing pipeline using Amazon MSK (Managed Streaming for Apache Kafka) and AWS Lambda to process data and store it in Amazon DynamoDB.
+      4: 
+      5: ## Architecture Overview
+      6: 
+      7: ![Architecture Diagram](https://via.placeholder.com/800x400?text=MSK+to+DynamoDB+Architecture)
+      8: 
+      9: This solution implements a complete data pipeline with the following components:
+     10: 
+     11: 1. **Infrastructure Layer**:
+     12:    - Amazon VPC with public and private subnets
+     13:    - Amazon MSK cluster (Provisioned or Serverless)
+     14:    - EC2 instance with development tools and Amazon Kiro Web
+     15: 
+     16: 2. **Producer Layer**:
+     17:    - Java-based Kafka producer application
+     18:    - Generates fake person data using JavaFaker
+     19:    - Sends JSON messages to MSK
+     20: 
+     21: 3. **Processing Layer**:
+     22:    - AWS Lambda function triggered by MSK events
+     23:    - Processes Kafka messages using AWS Lambda Powertools
+     24:    - Stores data in DynamoDB
+     25: 
+     26: ## Quick Start

+     38:     ParameterKey=GitHubRepoURL,ParameterValue=https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git \

+     40: ```

+     48: ```

I'll provide you with a list of commands to push this code into a remote Git

bash
# Initialize a Git repository (if not already initialized)
git init

# Add all files to the staging area
git add .

# Commit the changes
git commit -m "Initial commit: MSK to DynamoDB end-to-end project"

# Add the remote repository URL (replace with your actual repository URL)
git remote add origin https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git

# Push the code to the main branch
# If your default branch is named 'master' instead of 'main', replace 'main' with 'master'
git push -u origin main


If you're using SSH authentication instead of HTTPS, use this remote URL format:
bash
git remote add origin git@github.com:yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git


If you need to create a new branch before pushing:
bash
# Create and switch to a new branch
git checkout -b develop

# Push the new branch to the remote repository
git push -u origin develop


If you encounter authentication issues with HTTPS, you might need to use a 
personal access token:
bash
# Set the remote URL with your personal access token
git remote set-url origin https://yourusername:your_personal_access_token@github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git


Remember to replace yourusername, msk-dynamodb-project, and 
your_personal_access_token with your actual GitHub username, repository name, 
and personal access token.

> I've created a comprehensive .gitignore file for your project that will ensure
only relevant files get pushed to GitHub. Here's what it includes:

### What's Excluded:

1. Build Artifacts:
   • Java compiled classes (*.class)
   • JAR, WAR, and other archive files
   • Maven target directories

2. AWS SAM Build Files:
   • .aws-sam directory
   • packaged.yaml
   • samconfig.toml (since this will be generated on the EC2 instance)

3. Local Development Files:
   • DynamoDB Local data files
   • Local environment configuration

4. IDE-specific Files:
   • IntelliJ IDEA files (.idea/, .iml)
   • Eclipse files (.project, .classpath)
   • VS Code files (.vscode/)
   • NetBeans files

5. OS-specific Files:
   • macOS (.DSStore)
   • Windows (Thumbs.db)
   • Linux temporary files

6. Logs and Temporary Files:
   • Log files
   • Temporary directories

7. Project-specific Files:
   • kafka-client.properties (since it contains environment-specific 
configuration)
   • env.json (contains local environment variables)

### What's Included:

1. Source Code:
   • Java source files
   • XML configuration files
   • YAML templates

2. Documentation:
   • README.md files
   • Documentation resources

3. Templates:
   • I've added a kafka-client.properties.template file that will be included in
the repository
   • This template can be used as a reference for creating the actual properties
file

This .gitignore configuration ensures that your repository remains clean and 
only contains the necessary files for someone else to clone and use the project.
The environment-specific configuration will be generated on the EC2 instance 
during deployment, keeping sensitive information out of the repository.

> I've fixed the CloudFormation template and saved it as 
msk-environment-template-fixed.yaml in your project directory. Here are the key 
changes I made:

1. Fixed the YAML formatting issues: The main problem was in the UserData section
where the heredoc syntax was causing issues. I've properly formatted all the 
heredoc sections with consistent indentation.

2. Fixed the multiline script blocks: I've added single quotes around the heredoc
markers ('EOL') to ensure the content is treated as literal text, which 
prevents variable substitution within the heredoc blocks where needed.

3. Corrected indentation: Ensured consistent indentation throughout the template,
which is crucial for YAML parsing.

4. Improved variable handling: Made sure environment variables are properly 
substituted in the scripts.

The error you encountered (
can not read a block mapping entry; a multiline key may not be an implicit key) 
was specifically related to the comment line 
# Update Kafka producer properties file and the following heredoc syntax. In 
YAML, proper indentation is critical, especially when using multiline scripts.

To use the fixed template:

1. Upload the new msk-environment-template-fixed.yaml file to CloudFormation 
instead of the original file.

2. CloudFormation should now be able to parse the template without the 
formatting error.

3. The template will create all the necessary resources for your MSK to DynamoDB
project, including:
   • VPC with public and private subnets
   • MSK cluster (Provisioned or Serverless based on your parameter choice)
   • EC2 development instance with all required tools
   • DynamoDB table
   • Necessary security groups and IAM roles

> Let's look at the EC2Instance resource where the DependsOn attribute is likely
causing the issue:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_read (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Reading file: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-environment-template-fixed.yaml, from line 260 to 280
 ⋮ 
 ● Completed in 0.0s


> I found the issue! The problem is in the DependsOn attribute of the 
EC2Instance resource. The DependsOn attribute expects a string or a list of 
strings, but you're using an !If function directly inside it, which isn't 
allowed in this context.

 ↳ Purpose: Fixed the DependsOn attribute in the EC2Instance resource to properly handle conditional dependencies based on MSK cluster type.
Creating: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-environment-template-fixed-v2.yaml

 ⋮ 
 ● Completed in 0.2s


> I've fixed the CloudFormation template and saved it as 
msk-environment-template-fixed-v2.yaml. Here's what I changed to address the 
DependsOn error:

### Key Changes:

1. Split the EC2 Instance into two conditional resources:
   • Created EC2InstanceProvisioned (used when MSKClusterType = Provisioned)
   • Created EC2InstanceServerless (used when MSKClusterType = Serverless)

2. Fixed the DependsOn attribute:
   • For EC2InstanceProvisioned, set DependsOn: MSKClusterProvisioned
   • For EC2InstanceServerless, set DependsOn: MSKClusterServerless

3. Updated the Outputs section:
   • Modified the EC2InstanceId and EC2PublicIP outputs to use conditional logic
with !If to reference the correct EC2 instance based on the MSK cluster type

### Why This Fixes the Issue:

The error "Every DependsOn value must be a string" occurred because you were 
trying to use an !If function directly inside the DependsOn attribute. 
CloudFormation requires DependsOn values to be literal strings (resource names),
not intrinsic functions.

By creating separate EC2 instance resources for each MSK cluster type and using 
conditions to control which one gets created, we avoid the need to use !If 
inside the DependsOn attribute. Each EC2 instance now has a direct string 
reference to its corresponding MSK cluster.

### How to Use the Fixed Template:

1. Upload the new msk-environment-template-fixed-v2.yaml file to CloudFormation.

2. When creating the stack, choose either "Provisioned" or "Serverless" for the 
MSKClusterType parameter.

3. CloudFormation will create the appropriate MSK cluster type and the 
corresponding EC2 instance with the correct dependencies.

This approach ensures that the EC2 instance will only be created after its 
dependent MSK cluster is ready, regardless of which type you choose.

> Let's look for the Fn::Sub syntax issues in the template. The error message 
indicates that there are invalid characters in the variable names used within 
the !Sub function. This typically happens in the UserData sections where shell 
variables and CloudFormation variables might be mixed.

Let's examine the UserData sections in the template:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_read (trusted)
 ⋮

 ● Completed in 0.0s


> I found the issue! The problem is in the samconfig.toml file that's being 
created in the UserData script. The template is using ${AWS::Region} inside a 
heredoc, but it's also using shell variables like ${MSK_CLUSTER_ARN} and 
${KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME} which are causing conflicts with CloudFormation's Fn::Sub 
function.

Let's create a fixed version of the template:

 ⋮ 
 ↳ Purpose: Fixed the Fn::Sub syntax issues in the CloudFormation template by properly escaping shell variables in the UserData sections.
Creating: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-environment-template-fixed-v3.yaml

 ⋮ 
 ● Completed in 0.0s


> I've fixed the CloudFormation template and saved it as 
msk-environment-template-fixed-v3.yaml. Here are the key changes I made to 
address the Fn::Sub syntax error:

### Key Changes:

1. Fixed the heredoc syntax in the samconfig.toml file:
   • Removed the single quotes around the EOL marker to allow variable 
substitution
   • Properly escaped the shell variables in the parameter_overrides line using 
double backslashes
   • Changed from:
    

     parameter_overrides = "MSKClusterArn=\"${MSK_CLUSTER_ARN}\" KafkaTopicName=\"${KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME}\" Environment=\"dev\" DynamoDBTableName=\"PersonData\""
     

   • To:
    

     parameter_overrides = "MSKClusterArn=\\"$MSK_CLUSTER_ARN\\" KafkaTopicName=\\"$KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME\\" Environment=\\"dev\\" DynamoDBTableName=\\"PersonData\\""
     


2. Fixed the Kafka properties file heredoc:
   • Removed the single quotes around the EOL marker to allow variable 
substitution
   • This ensures that the shell variable $BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS gets properly 
expanded

### Why This Fixes the Issue:

The error "variable names in Fn::Sub syntax must contain only alphanumeric 
characters, underscores, periods, and colons" occurred because CloudFormation 
was trying to interpret shell variables like ${MSK_CLUSTER_ARN} as 
CloudFormation variables within the !Sub function.

In CloudFormation, when you use !Sub (or Fn::Sub), any ${VarName} pattern is 
interpreted as a reference to a CloudFormation variable or resource. However, in
shell scripts, the same syntax is used for shell variables.

The solution is to:
1. Use proper escaping for shell variables when they appear in a CloudFormation 
!Sub context
2. Control when variable substitution happens in heredocs by using or omitting 
quotes around the heredoc markers

### How to Use the Fixed Template:

1. Upload the new msk-environment-template-fixed-v3.yaml file to CloudFormation.

2. When creating the stack, choose either "Provisioned" or "Serverless" for the 
MSKClusterType parameter.

3. CloudFormation should now be able to parse the template without any syntax 
errors.

This template properly handles both CloudFormation variable substitution and 
shell variable substitution in the UserData scripts, which should resolve the 
error you were encountering.

variable references.

 ↳ Purpose: Created a completely fixed CloudFormation template that properly handles all shell variables in UserData sections.
Creating: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-environment-template-final.yaml

 ⋮ 
 ● Completed in 0.0s


> I've created a completely fixed CloudFormation template named 
msk-environment-template-final.yaml. Here are the key changes I made to address 
the persistent Fn::Sub syntax errors:

### Key Changes:

1. Changed heredoc markers from 'EOL' to 'EOF':
   • Using a different heredoc marker to avoid any potential conflicts

2. Added an AWS_REGION variable:
   • Created a shell variable AWS_REGION=${AWS::Region} to use in the scripts
   • This avoids using CloudFormation variables directly in the heredoc content

3. Improved the structure of the UserData section:
   • Made sure the Fn::Base64 and !Sub functions are properly nested
   • Ensured proper indentation throughout the script

4. Fixed all shell variable references:
   • Made sure shell variables like $MSK_CLUSTER_ARN and $KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME are 
properly handled
   • Used proper escaping for quotes in the parameter_overrides line

5. Used unquoted heredoc markers:
   • Removed quotes around EOF markers to allow variable substitution
   • Used quoted EOF markers ('EOF') only where variable substitution should be 
prevented

### Why This Should Fix the Issue:

The error "variable names in Fn::Sub syntax must contain only alphanumeric 
characters, underscores, periods, and colons" occurs when CloudFormation tries 
to interpret shell variables as CloudFormation variables. The key to fixing this
is to:

1. Make sure CloudFormation variables (like ${AWS::Region}) are only used in the
context where CloudFormation expects them
2. Properly handle shell variables to prevent CloudFormation from trying to 
interpret them
3. Use the correct heredoc syntax to control when variable substitution happens

This template carefully separates CloudFormation variable substitution from 
shell variable substitution, which should resolve the error.

### How to Use the Fixed Template:

1. Upload the new msk-environment-template-final.yaml file to CloudFormation.

2. When creating the stack, choose either "Provisioned" or "Serverless" for the 
MSKClusterType parameter.

3. CloudFormation should now be able to parse the template without any syntax 
errors.

If you still encounter issues with this template, we may need to take a more 
drastic approach by:
1. Moving the complex UserData scripts to separate files
2. Using CloudFormation Init (cfn-init) instead of UserData for complex 
configuration
3. Or using a simpler approach with fewer variable substitutions

> I've created a simplified template called msk-environment-simple.yaml. Let's 
test if this basic template works first, then we'll gradually add complexity.

Now, let's create a script that will handle the complex setup separately from 
the CloudFormation template:


🛠️  Using tool: fs_write (trusted)
 ⋮ 
 ● Path: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/setup-msk-environment.sh

+      1: #!/bin/bash -xe
+      2: 
+      3: # This script sets up the MSK to DynamoDB environment on an EC2 instance
+      4: # It should be downloaded and executed by the EC2 instance's UserData script
+      5: 
+      6: # Parameters (these will be passed as arguments)
+      7: STACK_NAME=$1
+      8: KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME=$2
+      9: GITHUB_REPO_URL=$3
+     10: AWS_REGION=$4
+     11: 
+     12: # Set environment variables
+     13: MSK_CLUSTER_ARN=$(aws cloudformation describe-stacks --stack-name $STACK_NAME --query "Stacks[0].Outputs[?OutputKey=='MSKClusterArn'].OutputValue" --output text)
+     14: 
+     15: # Get bootstrap servers
+     16: BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS=$(aws kafka get-bootstrap-brokers --cluster-arn $MSK_CLUSTER_ARN --query 'BootstrapBrokerStringSaslIam' --output text)
+     17: 
+     18: # Create Kafka topic
+     19: aws kafka create-topic --cluster-arn $MSK_CLUSTER_ARN --topic-name $KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME --number-of-partitions 3 --replication-factor 2
+     20: 
+     21: # Create DynamoDB table
+     22: aws dynamodb create-table \
+     23:   --table-name PersonData \
+     24:   --attribute-definitions AttributeName=id,AttributeType=S \
+     25:   --key-schema AttributeName=id,KeyType=HASH \
+     26:   --billing-mode PAY_PER_REQUEST
+     27: 
+     28: # Create project directory
+     29: mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/project
+     30: cd /home/ec2-user/project
+     31: 
+     32: # Clone repository
+     33: git clone $GITHUB_REPO_URL .
+     34: 
+     35: # Create build and run script for Kafka producer
+     36: cat > /home/ec2-user/project/kafka-producer/build-and-run.sh << 'EOF'
+     37: #!/bin/bash
+     38: 
+     39: TOPIC=${1:-person-data}
+     40: COUNT=${2:-10}
+     41: BATCH_ID=${3:-batch-$(date +%s)}
+     42: 
+     43: mvn clean package
+     44: 
+     45: java -jar target/kafka-producer-1.0-SNAPSHOT-jar-with-dependencies.jar \
+     46:   --properties kafka-client.properties \
+     47:   --topic $TOPIC \
+     48:   --count $COUNT \
+     49:   --batch-id $BATCH_ID
+     50: EOF
+     51: 
+     52: chmod +x /home/ec2-user/project/kafka-producer/build-and-run.sh
+     53: 
+     54: # Create SAM config file
+     55: mkdir -p /home/ec2-user/project/msk-dynamodb-processor
+     56: cat > /home/ec2-user/project/msk-dynamodb-processor/samconfig.toml << EOF
+     57: version = 0.1
+     58: [default]
+     59: [default.deploy]
+     60: [default.deploy.parameters]
+     61: stack_name = "msk-dynamodb-processor"
+     62: s3_bucket = "aws-sam-cli-managed-default-samclisourcebucket-EXAMPLE"
+     63: s3_prefix = "msk-dynamodb-processor"
+     64: region = "$AWS_REGION"
+     65: confirm_changeset = true
+     66: capabilities = "CAPABILITY_IAM"
+     67: parameter_overrides = "MSKClusterArn=\"$MSK_CLUSTER_ARN\" KafkaTopicName=\"$KAFKA_TOPIC_NAME\" Environment=\"dev\" DynamoDBTableName=\"PersonData\""
+     68: image_repositories = []
+     69: EOF
+     70: 
+     71: # Update Kafka producer properties file
+     72: cat > /home/ec2-user/project/kafka-producer/kafka-client.properties << EOF
+     73: # Kafka Producer Configuration
+     74: 
+     75: # Bootstrap servers
+     76: bootstrap.servers=$BOOTSTRAP_SERVERS
+     77: 
+     78: # MSK IAM Authentication
+     79: security.protocol=SASL_SSL
+     80: sasl.mechanism=AWS_MSK_IAM
+     81: sasl.jaas.config=software.amazon.msk.auth.iam.IAMLoginModule required;
+     82: sasl.client.callback.handler.class=software.amazon.msk.auth.iam.IAMClientCallbackHandler
+     83: 
+     84: # Producer specific settings
+     85: key.serializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer
+     86: value.serializer=org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer
+     87: EOF
+     88: 
+     89: # Create setup script
+     90: cat > /home/ec2-user/setup-project.sh << 'EOF'
+     91: #!/bin/bash
+     92: 
+     93: cd ~/project/kafka-producer
+     94: mvn clean package
+     95: 
+     96: echo "Project setup complete!"
+     97: echo "To run the Kafka producer:"
+     98: echo "  cd ~/project/kafka-producer"
+     99: echo "  ./build-and-run.sh person-data 100 batch-001"
+    100: echo ""
+    101: echo "To deploy the Lambda function:"
+    102: echo "  cd ~/project/msk-dynamodb-processor"
+    103: echo "  sam deploy --guided"
+    104: EOF
+    105: 
+    106: chmod +x /home/ec2-user/setup-project.sh
+    107: 
+    108: # Set ownership
+    109: chown -R ec2-user:ec2-user /home/ec2-user/project
+    110: chown ec2-user:ec2-user /home/ec2-user/setup-project.sh
+    111: 
+    112: echo "Setup script completed successfully"

+    390:     Description: Public IP of the EC2 Instance
+    391:     Value: !If [IsProvisionedMSK, !GetAtt EC2InstanceProvisioned.PublicIp, !GetAtt EC2InstanceServerless.PublicIp]
+    392:     
+    393:   KeyPairName:
+    394:     Description: Name of the Key Pair for SSH access
+    395:     Value: !Ref KeyPair
+    396:     
+    397:   KeyPairSecret:
+    398:     Description: Secret material of the Key Pair (save this securely)
+    399:     Value: !GetAtt KeyPair.PrivateKey

> I've taken a completely different approach to solve the CloudFormation 
template issues. Here's what I've created:

1. A simplified CloudFormation template (msk-environment-simple.yaml):
   • Contains only basic resources with minimal UserData
   • Useful for testing if the template syntax is valid

2. A separate setup script (setup-msk-environment.sh):
   • Contains all the complex configuration that was causing issues in the 
UserData
   • Will be downloaded and executed by the EC2 instance

3. An updated CloudFormation template (msk-environment-with-s3.yaml):
   • Uses S3 to host the setup script
   • Has a much simpler UserData section that just downloads and runs the script
   • Passes parameters to the script as command-line arguments

### How to Use This Solution:

1. Upload the setup script to an S3 bucket:
  
bash
   aws s3 cp setup-msk-environment.sh s3://your-bucket-name/setup-msk-environment.sh
   


2. Deploy the CloudFormation template:
   • Upload msk-environment-with-s3.yaml to CloudFormation
   • Provide the S3 bucket name as the SetupScriptS3Bucket parameter
   • Provide the S3 key as the SetupScriptS3Key parameter (default is 
setup-msk-environment.sh)

### Why This Approach Works:

1. Separation of concerns:
   • The CloudFormation template focuses on infrastructure provisioning
   • The setup script handles complex configuration

2. Avoids variable substitution issues:
   • The UserData section is much simpler with fewer variables
   • Shell variables are handled in the separate script, not in the 
CloudFormation template

3. Easier to debug and maintain:
   • You can update the setup script without changing the CloudFormation 
template
   • You can test the script independently

This approach should resolve the persistent Fn::Sub syntax errors you've been 
encountering. If you still have issues, we can try an even simpler approach or 
use CloudFormation Init (cfn-init) instead of UserData.

 ↳ Purpose: Fixed the CloudFormation template by removing the invalid KeyPairSecret output that was trying to access the PrivateKey attribute.
Creating: /Users/ibanerj/Documents/AmazonQDeveloperProjects/QDeveloperProjects/MSK_DynamoDB/Lambda/Java/msk-environment-with-s3-fixed.yaml

 ⋮ 
 ● Completed in 0.1s


> I've fixed the CloudFormation template and saved it as 
msk-environment-with-s3-fixed.yaml. Here's what I changed:

### The Issue:

The error occurred because the template was trying to output the private key 
material of the EC2 KeyPair using !GetAtt KeyPair.PrivateKey. However, the 
PrivateKey attribute is not available as an output from the AWS::EC2::KeyPair 
resource in CloudFormation.

### The Fix:

I removed the problematic output:
yaml
KeyPairSecret:
  Description: Secret material of the Key Pair (save this securely)
  Value: !GetAtt KeyPair.PrivateKey


### Important Note About EC2 KeyPairs:

When you create an EC2 KeyPair through CloudFormation, AWS doesn't provide a way
to retrieve the private key material after creation. This is a security 
feature. Instead, you have two options:

1. Import an existing key pair that you already have the private key for
2. Create the key pair outside of CloudFormation (e.g., using the AWS Console or 
CLI) and then reference it by name in your template

### How to Access Your EC2 Instance:

Since the private key won't be available through CloudFormation outputs, you'll 
need to:

1. Create a key pair manually before deploying the stack:
  
bash
   aws ec2 create-key-pair --key-name MSKEnvironment-KeyPair --query 'KeyMaterial' --output text > MSKEnvironment-KeyPair.pem
   chmod 400 MSKEnvironment-KeyPair.pem
   


2. Or modify the template to use an existing key pair by changing the KeyPair 
resource to:
  
yaml
   KeyPair:
     Type: AWS::EC2::KeyPair
     Properties:
       KeyName: your-existing-key-pair-name
   


### Next Steps:

1. Upload the setup script to an S3 bucket:
  
bash
   aws s3 cp setup-msk-environment.sh s3://your-bucket-name/setup-msk-environment.sh
   


2. Deploy the fixed CloudFormation template:
  
bash
   aws cloudformation create-stack \
     --stack-name msk-dynamodb-infrastructure \
     --template-body file://msk-environment-with-s3-fixed.yaml \
     --parameters \
       ParameterKey=EnvironmentName,ParameterValue=MSKEnvironment \
       ParameterKey=KafkaTopicName,ParameterValue=person-data \
       ParameterKey=MSKClusterType,ParameterValue=Provisioned \
       ParameterKey=GitHubRepoURL,ParameterValue=https://github.com/yourusername/msk-dynamodb-project.git \
       ParameterKey=SetupScriptS3Bucket,ParameterValue=your-bucket-name \
       ParameterKey=SetupScriptS3Key,ParameterValue=setup-msk-environment.sh \
     --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM
   


This fixed template should now deploy successfully without the error about the 
PrivateKey attribute.
